row 28 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = In some previous years, CBO published long-term projections for Social Security in a visual report (for example, CBO’s 2024 Long-Term Projections for Social Security), which included figures and tables along with additional information in the supplemental data. This year, the agency is not publishing a separate visual report but is releasing the data that would have been included in it. The data in Sheets 1 through 16 in this file correspond to the supplemental data published with previous visual reports. The data in appendix tables A-1 and A-2 correspond to the data underlying Figures 15 and 16 in the 2024 report. CBO's 2024 long-term projections for Social Security are posted on the landing page for CBO’s 2024 Long-Term Projections for Social Security.

row 127 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = Scheduled benefits are benefits as calculated under the Social Security Act, regardless of the balances of the trust funds. Taxable payroll is total earnings subject to the Social Security payroll tax. Tax revenues consist of payroll taxes, income taxes on benefits, and reimbursements from the general fund of the Treasury to make up for reductions in payroll taxes in 2011 and 2012. Outlays consist of scheduled benefits and administrative costs.

row 130 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = 
row 131 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = Tax revenues consist of payroll taxes, income taxes on benefits, and reimbursements from the general fund of the Treasury to make up for reductions in payroll taxes in 2011 and 2012. Tax revenues shown are consistent with payable benefits (they would be slightly higher if scheduled benefits were paid because revenues from income taxes paid on those benefits would be higher). Outlays consist of benefits and administrative costs.
row 132 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = 
row 133 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = 
row 134 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = 
row 135 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = 
row 136 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = Scheduled benefits are benefits as calculated under the Social Security Act, regardless of the balances of the trust funds. Payable benefits are scheduled benefits reduced as necessary to ensure that outlays do not exceed the Social Security system’s revenues once the combined balance of the Social Security trust funds is exhausted. If a trust fund’s balance declined to zero and current revenues were insufficient to cover benefits specified in law, the Social Security Administration would no longer be permitted to pay full benefits when they were due. In the years after a trust fund's balance was exhausted, annual outlays would be limited to annual revenues.

row 21 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = Data source: Congressional Budget Office.
row 22 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = 
row 23 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = Scheduled benefits are benefits as calculated under the Social Security Act, regardless of the balances of the trust funds. The actuarial balance is the difference between the income rate and the cost rate. Over the relevant periods, the income rate is the present value of a trust fund's annual income plus its initial balance, and the cost rate is the present value of annual outlays plus the present value of a year's worth of benefits at the end of the period. (A present-value estimate translates a flow of current and future income or payments into an equivalent lump-sum value today.) The income rate and the cost rate are divided by the present value of gross domestic product or of taxable payroll (total earnings subject to the Social Security payroll tax).

row 129 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = 
row 130 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = A trust fund ratio is the balance of the trust fund at the beginning of the calendar year divided by outlays (benefits and administrative costs) for that year. A trust fund's balance is exhausted when the ratio reaches zero. Under current law, a Social Security trust fund cannot incur a negative balance. Although the trust funds for Old-Age and Survivors Insurance (OASI) and Disability Insurance (DI) are legally separate, CBO generally follows the common analytical convention of considering them as combined and refers to them as the combined, or OASDI, trust funds.

row 92 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = a. Payroll tax contributions to the trust funds consist of taxes paid by employees, employers, and self-employed people on earnings covered by Social Security. 
row 93 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = 
row 94 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = b. "Taxation of benefits" refers to income taxes that some recipients pay on their Social Security benefits.

row 96 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = c. No interest is calculated on a trust fund after its balance is projected to be exhausted.

row 89 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = Data source: Congressional Budget Office.
row 90 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = 
row 91 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = Generally, a trust fund has a surplus if the net cash flow is positive and a deficit if the net cash flow is negative. When the net cash flow is negative and there are not enough assets in the trust fund to finance it, the balance of the trust fund is considered exhausted. CBO calls the excess benefits assumed to be paid in that situation a shortfall. A primary deficit or shortfall is the deficit or shortfall excluding interest paid to the trust fund.
row 92 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = 
row 93 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = CBO projects that the balance of the Old-Age and Survivors Insurance (OASI) Trust Fund will be exhausted in fiscal year 2032 and that the balance of the Disability Insurance (DI) Trust Fund will be exhausted sometime after 2100. Under current law, the Social Security Administration (SSA) may not pay benefits in excess of the available balance of a trust fund, borrow money for a trust fund, or transfer money from one trust fund to another. However, following rules in section 257(b) of the Deficit Control Act of 1985, CBO's baseline reflects the assumption that SSA will continue to pay OASI and DI benefits in full even after the balances of the trust funds are exhausted.

row 32 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = 
row 33 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = Scheduled benefits are benefits as calculated under the Social Security Act, regardless of the balances of the trust funds. Payable benefits are scheduled benefits reduced as necessary to ensure that outlays do not exceed the Social Security system’s revenues once the combined balance of the Social Security trust funds is exhausted.

row 28 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = 
row 29 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = Data source: Congressional Budget Office.
row 30 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = 
row 31 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = The initial replacement rate for retired workers is the initial annual benefit amount measured as a percentage of workers' preretirement earnings, defined as the average of the last five years of substantial earnings before age 62. (Substantial earnings are annual earnings that amount to at least half of a worker's average indexed annual earnings.) To compute preretirement earnings, past earnings are adjusted for average growth in prices. Replacement rates are computed for all people who are eligible to claim retirement benefits at age 62 and who have not yet claimed other Social Security benefits. To include only people with significant attachment to the labor force, CBO excluded workers with fewer than 20 years of earnings equal to at least 10 percent of average wages in the total economy in each year. All workers are assumed to claim benefits at age 65. All values are net of income taxes paid on benefits.
row 32 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = 
row 33 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = Scheduled benefits are benefits as calculated under the Social Security Act, regardless of the balances of the trust funds. Payable benefits are scheduled benefits reduced as necessary to ensure that outlays do not exceed the Social Security system’s revenues once the combined balance of the Social Security trust funds is exhausted.

row 35 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = A quintile is one-fifth of the distribution of lifetime household earnings.

row 30 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = 
row 31 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = Benefits are measured as the present value of all retired-worker benefits received. Benefits are computed for all people who claim retired-worker benefits on the basis of their own earnings and who have not yet claimed any other Social Security benefits. (A present-value estimate translates a flow of current and future income or payments into an equivalent lump-sum value today.) To calculate the present value, benefits are adjusted to remove the effects of inflation and discounted to age 65. All values are net of income taxes paid on benefits.

Payable benefits are scheduled benefits reduced as necessary to ensure that outlays do not exceed the Social Security system’s revenues once the combined balance of the Social Security trust funds is exhausted.

row 19 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = 
row 20 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = a. Initial annual benefits are computed for people projected to receive Disability Insurance (DI) worker benefits who live past age 45.
row 21 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = 
row 22 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = b. The initial replacement rate for disabled workers is the initial annual benefit amount measured as a percentage of the average of the worker's last five years of substantial earnings before being awarded benefits. (Substantial earnings are annual earnings that amount to at least half of a worker's average indexed annual earnings.) To compute the last five years' substantial earnings, past earnings are adjusted for average growth in prices. Initial replacement rates are computed for people projected to receive DI worker benefits who live past age 45. To include only people with significant attachment to the labor force, CBO excluded workers with fewer than five years of substantial earnings. All values are net of income taxes paid on benefits.
row 23 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = 
row 24 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = c. Lifetime benefits consist of the present value of all disability benefits received plus retired-worker benefits received after the full retirement age. To calculate the present value, benefits are adjusted to remove the effects of inflation and discounted to age 65. (A present-value estimate translates a flow of current and future income or payments into an equivalent lump-sum value today.) To compute lifetime earnings, past earnings are adjusted for average growth in prices.

row 45 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = 
row 46 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = The benefit-to-tax ratio is a comparison of the present value of benefits received over a lifetime (net of income taxes that some recipients pay on their benefits) and the present value of payroll taxes paid over a lifetime. (A present-value estimate translates a flow of current and future income or payments into an equivalent lump-sum value today.) Lifetime Social Security benefits consist of the present value of all benefits except those received by young or disabled widows, young or disabled spouses, and children, which are excluded from this measure because of insufficient data for years before 1984. Lifetime Social Security payroll taxes consist of the present value of the employer’s and employee’s shares of payroll taxes. To calculate the present value, amounts are adjusted to remove the effects of inflation and discounted to age 65.

row 48 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = Scheduled benefits are benefits as calculated under the Social Security Act, regardless of the balances of the trust funds. Payable benefits are scheduled benefits reduced as necessary to ensure that outlays do not exceed the Social Security system’s revenues once the combined balance of the Social Security trust funds is exhausted.

row 15 | CBO's 2026 Long-Term Projections for Social Security = Lifetime Social Security benefits consist of the present value of all benefits except those received by young or disabled widows, young or disabled spouses, and children, which are excluded from this measure because of insufficient data for years before 1984. (A present-value estimate translates a flow of current and future income or payments into an equivalent lump-sum value today.) To calculate the present value, amounts are adjusted to remove the effects of inflation and discounted to age 65.
